Search|t Updates Countries Disasters Organizations Topics Jobs Training Informing humanitarians worldwide 24/7 — a service provided by UN OCHA World + 8 more Epidemic and emerging disease alerts in the Pacific as of 25 August 2026 Format Map Source SPC Posted 25 Aug 2026 Originally published 25 Aug 2026 Attachments Download Map (PDF | 636.45 KB) Highlights/updates since the last map was sent on PacNet on 18 August 2026: Dengue • American Samoa: As of 11 August 2026, a cumulative total of 1,044 laboratory-confirmed dengue cases has been recorded since the start of the outbreak. Weekly counts remain low, two cases in EpiWeek 30, two in EpiWeek 31 and three in EpiWeek 32. Monthly counts have declined continuously since the January 2026 peak of about 175 cases. Cases concentrate in the 11–15 and 16–20-year age groups. The blue alert for dengue (DENV-1 & DENV-2) remains in effect. – Source: American Samoa Department of Health, Dengue Response Incident Command Briefing, 13 August 2026, shared with the PPHSN Coordinating Body Focal point on the 20 August 2026. • Marshall Islands: On 21 August 2026, the Ministry of Health and Human Services formally declared an active dengue fever outbreak in Majuro Atoll. The event was first identified on 15 August through routine dengue-like illness surveillance and escalated to a full outbreak response. As of 20 August, 23 people had been tested, 8 confirmed positive and 15 negative, with no deaths. Two of the initial positive samples were sent to the Hawaii State Laboratories Division for serotyping and results are pending. Cases cluster in the 20–29-year age group. The response is coordinated under the Ministry's EpiNet framework with the National Disaster Council and covers active case finding, vector control, laboratory referral and public health messaging. Larvicide was applied on 18 August around the first four patients' residences in Uliga and Alwal, where inadequately sealed water catchments and a nearby landfill were identified as breeding risks. A red alert for dengue (DENV-?) is issued for the Marshall Islands. – Sources: RMI Ministry of Health and Human Services press release, 21 August 2026 - Official Facebook page, and Dengue-Like Illness in Majuro, Republic of the Marshall Islands - Official Facebook page accessed on 25 August 2026. • New Caledonia: On 16 August 2026, the Direction des Affaires sanitaires et sociales declared the dengue epidemic over. Since 1st January 2026, 2,235 dengue cases have been reported, including 2,028 confirmed locally acquired. Eighty patients were hospitalised and four died, one co-infected with leptospirosis and three with comorbidities. Serotype 1 (DENV-1) remained the only serotype identified throughout. The territory entered the epidemic phase on 26 March 2026 (EpiWeek 13) and new cases have declined steadily since EpiWeek 20. The epidemic mainly spared Greater Nouméa, where the World Mosquito Program has established the bacterium Wolbachia in 80% of mosquitoes. Low-level circulation persists and vigilance is maintained. The dengue (DENV-1) alert for New Caledonia is removed. – Sources: Bilan épidémiologique – fin de l'épidémie de dengue, 16 August 2026 shared with the PPHSN Coordinating Body Focal point 21 August 2026 and La dengue | Direction des Affaires Sanitaires et Sociales de Nouvelle-Calédonie accessed on 25 August 2026. • Samoa: In EpiWeek 32 (3–9 August 2026), 22 new dengue cases were reported, an increase of 47% on the previous week. Three were laboratory-confirmed, a 50% decrease in confirmed cases. No new hospital admissions, no intensive care admissions and no deaths were reported. Since 1st January 2025, a cumulative total of 20,157 clinically diagnosed and 5,536 laboratory-confirmed cases has been reported, with nine dengue-related deaths. Both serotype 1 (DENV-1, 52%) and serotype 2 (DENV-2, 48%) continue to circulate, 73% of cases are aged under 15 years and 92% are on Upolu. The blue alert for dengue (DENV-1 & DENV-2) remains in effect. – Source: Dengue Fever Outbreak Situation Report in Samoa, EpiWeek 32, Issue No. 69 shared with the PPHSN Coordinating Body Focal point on the 21 August 2026. • Vanuatu: The dengue outbreak on South-West Efate Island, Shefa Province, remains active. In EpiWeek 33 (10–16 August 2026), 15 new laboratory-confirmed cases were reported, the highest weekly count of the outbreak, bringing the cumulative total to 91 confirmed cases. Weekly counts have stayed above the outbreak threshold of four cases per week since EpiWeek 24. Dengue-like illness reports from the six Shefa sentinel sites also peaked, at 31 cases. Fourteen dengue-related hospitalisations (15%) have been reported to date. All patients have recovered and been discharged. No severe dengue cases or deaths have been reported. Between EpiWeeks 14 and 33, 383 samples were tested with an overall positivity rate of 20%. DENV-1 is the only confirmed serotype. Cases are spread across Area Councils on Efate: Erakor 30%, Port Vila 23%, Eratap 21% and Pango 20%. Among the confirmed cases, 73% are people under 26 years, with the highest burden in children aged 5–15 (45%). While some initial cases reported recent travel to Fiji, most recent cases have no travel history and are linked to affected areas within Efate. The red alert for dengue (DENV-1) remains in effect. – Source: Dengue Outbreak Situation Report #10, South-West Efate Island, Shefa Province, Vanuatu National Surveillance Unit shared with the PPHSN Coordinating Body Focal point on the 21 August 2026. • Wallis and Futuna: The Agence de Santé reports that the dengue epidemic is still progressing. As of 21 August 2026, 2 new confirmed cases were recorded in Futuna, bringing the territory to 70 confirmed or probable cases since the epidemic began progressing in EpiWeek 26. Transmission is currently concentrated in Futuna, where dengue-like syndromes are also increasing; Wallis reported no new confirmed case this week. DENV-1 remains the only circulating serotype. The public health response is applied case by case: destruction of larval breeding sites around the home of every case, personal protection with repellents issued to all cases, and peri-focal mosquito control around the households of confirmed cases. The red alert for dengue (DENV-1) remains in effect. – Source: Agence de Santé de Wallis et Futuna, weekly epidemiological update for EpiWeek 33, shared with the PPHSN Coordinating Body Focal Point on 25 August 2026. Measles and rubella • Australia: The Northern Territory (NT) Centre for Disease Control issued a health alert to all clinicians on 17 August 2026, warning of an increased risk of measles in the Territory because of outbreaks interstate and overseas. According to the NT health alert, Australia has recorded 117 measles cases in 2026 year to date, well above the five-year national annual mean of 35.6, and most were acquired overseas. Clinicians are asked to consider measles in any patient presenting with fever and rash, particularly after travel in the previous 18 days, and to collect urine, throat and nose samples for PCR testing. The red alert for measles remains in effect for Australia. – Sources: Northern Territory Centre for Disease Control measles health alert, 17 August 2026 and Dashboard · NINDSS Portal accessed on 25 August 2026. • Papua New Guinea: The National Department of Health issued Situation Report #2 on the measles and rubella outbreak on 19 August 2026. Three laboratory-confirmed measles cases have been recorded to date: East Sepik (2) and New Ireland (1). Together with seven confirmed rubella cases in New Ireland (4), Madang (2) and East Sepik (1). There have been no hospitalisations and no deaths. Since January 2026, 209 acute fever and rash cases have been reported with samples collected from 166. Of the collected samples 95 were negative and 64 remain pending laboratory testing. Outbreak response immunisation has vaccinated 1,816 children (77%) in the affected Kairiru Island area of East Sepik and has expanded to the whole of Wewak District. Routine coverage remains low, leaving approximately 660,000 children unprotected against measles, nearly two annual birth cohorts. The red alert for measles and rubella remains in effect. – Source: Measles and Rubella Outbreak Situation Report #2, Papua New Guinea National Department of Health, endorsed 19 August 2026, shared with the PPHSN Coordinating Body Focal point on the 20 August 2026. Pertussis/Whooping cough • Guam: The Department of Public Health and Social Services has confirmed that the pertussis outbreak in Guam was declared over in June 2026. To address questions and provide further context on Guam's pertussis situation, DPHSS has published an epidemiological series outlining the available evidence and public health interpretation. As of 19 August 2026 the territory has recorded 19 pertussis cases in 2026, including one new laboratory-confirmed case that week, six previously hospitalised cases and three with an identified epidemiological link. DPHSS expects a few further sporadic cases over the remainder of the year. The red alert for pertussis in Guam is removed. – Sources: Guam DPHSS correspondence with the PPHSN Coordinating Body Focal point, 19 August 2026, Guam Department of Public Health and Social Services – Official Facebook page and Risk to Guam: an epi series, Guam DPHSS accessed on 25 August 2026. • New Zealand: Pertussis activity in New Zealand continues as part of the ongoing national epidemic declared in November 2024. In the surveillance week ending 14 August 2026, 50 cases were notified, remaining stable compared with the 48 cases reported in the previous week (ending 07 August 2026), bringing the cumulative total for 2026 to 1,237 cases as of 14 August 2026. Cases remain concentrated in the 5–14 year and 1–4-year age groups, which also account for most hospitalisations. The blue alert for pertussis remains in effect. – Source: Pertussis dashboard, PHF Science accessed on 25 August 2026. Respiratory diseases • New Zealand: The weekly all-cause hospitalisation rate for severe acute respiratory infection (SARI) in the Auckland region increased markedly in the week ending 16 August 2026 and entered the VERY HIGH activity band for the first time in 2026. The rate is the highest recorded in any week from 2015 to 2025. Sentinel hospital SARI activity identified 177 SARI admissions that week against 125 the previous week. The influenza-positive SARI hospitalisation rate is also in the very high band, its highest since the June 2022 peak. Influenza test positivity reached 53.4% in sentinel general practice samples and 42.1% in SARI samples, with 67 influenza detections in SARI. Influenza A(H3N2) Subclade K is the predominant circulating virus. Thirty-one respiratory illness outbreaks were reported nationally, 21 of them in aged residential care. Rates rose across all age groups and all ethnic groups, children aged 0–4 have the highest rate, and Māori and Pacific people higher rates than other groups. SARS-CoV-2 activity remains low and stable. A red alert for influenza (Inf-A) is issued for New Zealand. – Sources: New Zealand Institute for Public Health and Forensic Science (PHF Science) Acute Respiratory Illness Infections dashboard and Influenza in New Zealand - BEACON report 22 August 2026 accessed on 25 August 2026. Other Information: COVID-19 • Palau: The Ministry of Health and Human Services continues to respond to an increase in respiratory disease. As of 21 August 2026, a cumulative total of 183 COVID-19 cases has been reported since 1st January 2026, up from 162 as of 14 August (21 cases in one week) with 14 hospital admissions and 12 discharges. Palau reported in their weekly syndromic and laboratory surveillance report continued COVID-19 activity in EpiWeek 34 (17-23 August 2026), with 23 SARS-CoV-2 detections among 51 respiratory specimens tested. Overall respiratory test positivity reached 55%, the highest weekly level recorded in 2026, with SARS-CoV-2 accounting for 82% (23/28) of all positive detections. Although fewer specimens were tested compared with EpiWeek 33 (51 vs 93), the positivity rate increased. Rhinovirus/enterovirus (4 detections) was the only other pathogen identified, while no RSV was detected. Cumulative SARS-CoV-2 detections for 2026 reached 177. – Sources: Palau Ministry of Health and Human Services situation updates for tuberculosis, pertussis and COVID-19, 21 August 2026 accessed on 25 August 2026 and Ministry of Health and Human Services syndromic surveillance report, 2026 EpiWeek 34, dated 24 August 2026 shared with the PPHSN Coordinating Body Focal point on 24 August 2026. Highly Pathogenic Avian Influenza • Australia: On 23 August 2026, Australia confirmed its first H5N1 detection in a mammal: a long-nosed fur seal found dead with no signs of injury at Beachport, South Australia, about 310 km south of Adelaide. The state's fur seal population is estimated at 100,000. South Australia's chief veterinary officer noted that seals are at high risk because they live very near wild birds on rocky coastal outcrops. The death does not appear associated with other sick or dead marine mammals. As of 23 August, Australia had recorded 309 confirmed H5 positive events across six states: South Australia 194, Victoria 86, New South Wales 11, Western Australia 10, Tasmania 7 and Queensland 1. Three mass mortality events involving more than 1,000 crested terns were identified on remote offshore islands in South Australia, described by the state Premier as a significant escalation, and 28 dead penguins were found on King Island, Tasmania. Victoria is vaccinating little penguins on Phillip Island and New South Wales is planning vaccination of priority captive native species. H5N1 has not been detected in Australian poultry or livestock. These developments continue to highlight the risk of H5N1 introduction to the Pacific region through migratory seabirds. – Sources: Expanding HPAI (H5N1) epizootic in Australia- BEACON report 21 August 2026First confirmed HPAI (H5N1) infection in a long-nosed fur seal in South Australia – BEACON report 23 August 2026 and Bird flu (avian influenza) - DAFF accessed on 25 August 2026. • New Zealand: On 21st August 2026, the Ministry for Primary Industries confirmed H5N1 avian influenza clade 2.3.4.4b in a single northern giant petrel found dead on a remote beach at Cape Palliser in the Wairarapa. This is the third confirmed H5 detection in New Zealand, all three in the Wellington region and all in individual wild birds. There have been no mass mortality events and no detections in poultry. Samples reached the Animal Health Laboratory on 14 August and required several rounds of testing because of the low viral load. MPI staff carried out precautionary checks along the Wairarapa coastline the following day. The chief veterinary officer assesses the risk to human health as low and states that chicken and eggs remain safe to eat. – Source: New Zealand Ministry for Primary Industries media releases of 21 and 22 August 2026 see About bird flu and the risk to New Zealand, MPI accessed on 25 August 2026. Influenza • Australia: The New South Wales respiratory surveillance report for EpiWeek 33, ending 15 August 2026, records influenza activity still increasing and remaining at a high level. Emergency department presentations and hospital admissions for influenza-like illness both increased, including among all children under 10 years of age. In Victoria, the Chief Health Officer issued a health alert on 24 August 2026 on rising respiratory infections: influenza and COVID-19 are increasing while RSV remains at a moderate level following its peak in July. Health service visits for respiratory illness have also been increasing. The alert stresses that this is a later-than-usual seasonal increase compared with previous seasons, that it is not too late to be vaccinated, and that young children, pregnant women, older adults and immunocompromised people are at greater risk of severe disease. The Victorian Respiratory Surveillance Report of 21 August (week ending 15 August) showed a 42% increase in influenza notifications, rising from 1,168 to 1,655 cases. Test positivity also increased from 3.8% to 4.9%. COVID-19 notifications remained stable (605 compared with 582 the previous week), while RSV notifications declined by 13%, from 1,194 to 1,040 cases, with positivity rate decreasing from 7.5% to 6.3%. RSV activity has now been declining for six weeks among children under five years and for four weeks among adults aged 65 years and over. Modelling by the Australia-Aotearoa Consortium for Epidemic Forecasting and Analytics gives more than 99% probability that influenza notifications are increasing. Importantly, Victorian influenza activity is nonetheless rated only low-moderate and notified cases remain lower than at the same point in the past two years. – Sources: NSW Health respiratory surveillance report, epidemiological week 33 ending 15 August 2026, Victorian Chief Health Officer alert of 24 August 2026 and the Victorian Respiratory Surveillance Report accessed on 25 August 2026. Rotavirus • Tuvalu: The Ministry of Health issued its third rotavirus situation report, covering 17–22 August 2026. Twenty-three cases are now laboratory-confirmed, all of them in Funafuti and spread across all six districts of Fongafale, following the first positive result in a young child on 4 August. Children carry the burden: 18 of the 23 are under 15 years of age and 11 are under 5. One child has been admitted to hospital and no deaths have been reported. Syndromic surveillance describes a larger event than the confirmed count: total diarrhoea consultations peaked at 147 in EpiWeek 33 before falling back to 47 in EpiWeek 34, with 135 suspected cases recorded between 4 and 22 August. Time
Published 25 August 2026; event dates range from 1 January 2026 to 21 August 2026.
Location and population
American Samoa (territory of the United States), Marshall Islands (Majuro Atoll), New Caledonia, Samoa, Vanuatu (South-West Efate Island, Shefa Province), Wallis and Futuna.
Event
Multiple Pacific territories reported dengue outbreaks or elevated transmission with serotype-specific alerts: American Samoa (DENV-1 & DENV-2 blue alert), Marshall Islands (DENV-? red alert), Samoa (DENV-1 & DENV-2 blue alert), Vanuatu (DENV-1 red alert), Wallis and Futuna (DENV-1 red alert). New Caledonia declared the dengue epidemic over on 16 August 2026.
Scale, impact and risk
American Samoa: 1,044 laboratory-confirmed cases since outbreak start; weekly counts low (2–3 cases in EpiWeeks 30–32). Marshall Islands: 8 confirmed cases, 15 negative, no deaths; outbreak declared 21 August 2026. New Caledonia: 2,235 cases (2,028 locally acquired), 80 hospitalisations, 4 deaths (1 co-infected with leptospirosis, 3 with comorbidities); DENV-1 only; epidemic declared over. Samoa: 22 new cases in EpiWeek 32 (47% increase), 3 laboratory-confirmed; cumulative 20,157 clinically diagnosed and 5,536 laboratory-confirmed cases since 1 January 2025, 9 deaths; DENV-1 (52%) and DENV-2 (48%) circulating.
Response, status and uncertainty
American Samoa: Weekly counts declining since January 2026 peak; cases concentrated in 11–20-year age groups; blue alert remains. Marshall Islands: Active outbreak response coordinated under EpiNet with National Disaster Council; active case finding, vector control, laboratory referral, and public health messaging; larvicide applied around residences; serotyping pending. New Caledonia: Epidemic declared over; low-level circulation persists; Wolbachia intervention in Greater Nouméa. Samoa: Blue alert remains; both serotypes circulating; 73% of cases under 15 years.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

登革热病例上升:了解症状与警示体征 - NST Online

Dengue cases rise: Know the symptoms and warning signs - NST Online
新闻简报This article from NST Online, published on August 26, 2026, highlights the rising number of dengue cases and deaths in Malaysia. According to the Health Ministry, there were 42,848 dengue cases between January 1 and July 9, 2026, a 34.8 per cent increase compared to the same period in 2025. Dengue-related deaths also rose by 78.9 per cent, from 19 to 34. The piece emphasizes the importance of recognizing symptoms early, such as high fever, severe headache, and muscle pain, and seeking medical advice rather than relying on self-diagnosis. It also warns against using NSAIDs like aspirin and ibuprofen, which can increase the risk of bleeding. The article outlines the critical phase of dengue, where warning signs such as severe abdominal pain, persistent vomiting, and pale skin may appear after the fever subsides. It recommends home care measures, including rest, hydration, and avoiding mosquito bites, but stresses that medical attention is necessary if symptoms worsen. The article serves as educational guidance and does not provide specific outbreak details or containment measures.

事件
一篇文章讨论马来西亚登革热病例与死亡人数的上升,强调早期识别症状与警示体征的重要性以预防严重并发症。该文章为一般性信息指导,非暴发报告。
规模、影响与风险
Malaysia recorded 42,848 dengue cases between January 1 and July 9, 2026, a 34.8 per cent increase from 31,790 cases during the same period last year. Dengue-related deaths also rose 78.9 per cent, from 19 to 34 over the same period. The article highlights the risk of severe dengue, which can lead to severe bleeding, organ impairment, or shock.
应对、状态与不确定性
文章提供登革热症状管理、避免使用NSAIDs及出现警示体征时及时就医的一般性指导。未报告具体调查、检测或防控措施。内容基于卫生部数据,旨在提升公众意识。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue cases rise: Know the symptoms and warning signs - NST Online

News Brief
This article from NST Online, published on August 26, 2026, highlights the rising number of dengue cases and deaths in Malaysia. According to the Health Ministry, there were 42,848 dengue cases between January 1 and July 9, 2026, a 34.8 per cent increase compared to the same period in 2025. Dengue-related deaths also rose by 78.9 per cent, from 19 to 34. The piece emphasizes the importance of recognizing symptoms early, such as high fever, severe headache, and muscle pain, and seeking medical advice rather than relying on self-diagnosis. It also warns against using NSAIDs like aspirin and ibuprofen, which can increase the risk of bleeding. The article outlines the critical phase of dengue, where warning signs such as severe abdominal pain, persistent vomiting, and pale skin may appear after the fever subsides. It recommends home care measures, including rest, hydration, and avoiding mosquito bites, but stresses that medical attention is necessary if symptoms worsen. The article serves as educational guidance and does not provide specific outbreak details or containment measures.

Haze Nepal floods Roller coaster mishap Ayob Khan Dolly Parton 2027 Budget Yap Kim Hock Khairy Jamaluddin Tabung Haji RCI Pensonic CEO Dengue cases rise: Know the symptoms and warning signs By Dale John Wong August 26, 2026 @ 10:44pm As dengue cases and deaths rise in Malaysia, recognising the symptoms and warning signs early could prevent serious complications. - File pic credit (ECDC) MALAYSIA recorded 42,848 dengue cases between Jan 1 and July 9 this year, a 34.8 per cent increase from 31,790 cases during the same period last year, according to the Health Ministry. Dengue-related deaths also rose 78.9 per cent, from 19 to 34 over the same period. The figures underline the need to recognise dengue symptoms early, understand which medicines should be avoided and seek medical attention when warning signs emerge. HOW DENGUE SPREADS Dengue is a viral infection transmitted primarily through the bite of an infected female Aedes mosquito. It does not normally spread directly from one person to another. However, mosquitoes can acquire the virus by biting an infected person and later transmit it to others. Aedes mosquitoes, recognisable by the white markings on their bodies and legs, are most active during the day. Rainfall, warm temperatures and high humidity can support their breeding and survival, particularly when stagnant water is readily available. Dengue is caused by four virus serotypes: DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3 and DENV-4. Recovery from infection with one serotype generally provides long-term immunity only against that particular serotype. A person can therefore contract dengue more than once, with a subsequent infection carrying a higher risk of severe dengue. RECOGNISING SYMPTOMS Symptoms usually begin four to 10 days after infection and typically last two to seven days. Common symptoms include: • high fever of 40 ̊c • severe headache; • pain behind the eyes; • muscle and joint pain; • nausea or vomiting; • rash; and • fatigue. These symptoms can resemble those of other illnesses. Anyone who suspects dengue should seek medical advice rather than rely on self-diagnosis, as a doctor may need to assess the symptoms and conduct blood tests. THE CRITICAL PHASE One of the most important things to understand about dengue is that a falling temperature does not necessarily mean the patient is recovering. Warning signs of severe dengue often appear after the fever has subsided, generally during the critical phase of the illness. Immediate medical attention should be sought if the patient develops: • severe or worsening abdominal pain; • persistent vomiting; • bleeding from the nose or gums; • blood in vomit or stool; • rapid breathing; • restlessness or unusual fatigue; • pale or cold skin; • severe weakness; or • excessive thirst. Severe dengue can lead to severe bleeding, organ impairment or shock, and hospital treatment may be required when warning signs are present. MANAGING FEVER SAFELY There is no specific antiviral treatment for dengue. Medical care generally focuses on monitoring the patient's condition, maintaining hydration and safely relieving symptoms. The World Health Organisation recommends paracetamol to reduce fever and relieve pain associated with dengue. Aspirin, ibuprofen and other non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) should be avoided because they can increase the risk of bleeding. Patients should follow the dosage stated on the label or prescribed by a healthcare professional and avoid taking more than one medicine containing paracetamol at the same time. Anyone with an existing medical condition, taking other medication or uncertain about which medicine to use should consult a doctor or pharmacist. CARING AT HOME Patients who have been assessed by a doctor and advised to recover at home should rest, drink plenty of fluids and continue watching for warning signs. Water, oral rehydration solutions and other suitable fluids can help replace fluids lost through fever and vomiting. Reduced urination, a dry mouth, dizziness or unusual drowsiness may indicate dehydration and should not be ignored. Patients should also avoid further mosquito bites while ill. A mosquito that bites an infected person can acquire the virus and transmit it to someone else. Using mosquito repellent, wearing clothing that covers the arms and legs and resting under a mosquito net can help reduce this risk. Home care should not replace medical assessment. If symptoms worsen, the patient is unable to drink, vomiting persists or any warning sign appears, medical attention should be sought immediately. PREVENTING MOSQUITO BREEDING Preventing dengue requires action both inside and outside the home. Aedes mosquitoes can breed in relatively small amounts of stagnant water. Potential breeding sites include flowerpot trays, buckets, discarded containers, clogged drains, water storage vessels and other items that collect rainwater. Households can lower the risk by: • emptying, cleaning and scrubbing water-holding containers at least once a week; • covering water storage containers securely; • disposing of rubbish and unused containers properly; • clearing blocked drains and gutters; • wearing long-sleeved clothing where practical; • applying mosquito repellent containing DEET, picaridin or IR3535 according to the instructions; and • using window screens, mosquito nets, coils or vapour repellents where appropriate. Regular checks remain important even when there are no known dengue cases nearby, as mosquito populations and transmission patterns can change quickly. A REMINDER TO BE VIGILANT Malaysia's rising dengue cases and deaths show why the disease should not be dismissed as an ordinary fever. Recognising symptoms early, obtaining medical advice and watching closely for warning signs can reduce the risk of serious complications. For fever and body aches associated with suspected dengue, paracetamol is generally recommended when used correctly. Aspirin, ibuprofen and other NSAIDs should be avoided because of the potential bleeding risk. Most importantly, a patient whose fever is falling must still be monitored. In dengue, the period after the fever subsides can be when serious warning signs begin to appear. This article is for educational purposes and does not replace medical advice, diagnosis or treatment from a qualified healthcare professional. An article discusses the rise in dengue cases and deaths in Malaysia, emphasizing the importance of recognizing symptoms and warning signs early to prevent serious complications. The article is a general informational piece, not an outbreak report.
Scale, impact and risk
Malaysia recorded 42,848 dengue cases between January 1 and July 9, 2026, a 34.8 per cent increase from 31,790 cases during the same period last year. Dengue-related deaths also rose 78.9 per cent, from 19 to 34 over the same period. The article highlights the risk of severe dengue, which can lead to severe bleeding, organ impairment, or shock.
Response, status and uncertainty
The article provides general guidance on managing dengue symptoms, avoiding NSAIDs, and seeking medical attention when warning signs appear. It does not report on specific investigations, testing, or containment measures. The content is based on data from the Health Ministry and is intended for public awareness.

Monoclonal for Dengue Shows Promise in Hospitalized Cases - MedPage Today

Monoclonal for Dengue Shows Promise in Hospitalized Cases - MedPage Today

News Brief
A recent news report from MedPage Today highlights a phase II study that suggests preliminary evidence of benefit from monoclonal antibodies in treating hospitalized cases of dengue virus. The study, published on August 26, 2026, indicates that these antibodies may offer promise in combating the mosquito-borne infection. However, no specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are provided in the report. The findings are described as preliminary, and the report does not mention further details on the investigation or response measures. The study is part of a broader discussion on dengue treatment options, but no additional information on the scale of impact or risk levels is included. The report serves as a summary of the study's implications, emphasizing the potential of monoclonal antibodies in managing severe dengue cases.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

皮内拉斯县15年来首次记录本地感染登革热病例——圣彼得堡催化剂

Pinellas records first locally acquired dengue cases in more than 15 years - St Pete Catalyst
新闻简报根据佛罗里达州卫生部报告,佛罗里达州皮内拉斯县在超过15年后首次记录了两例本地感染登革热病例。确诊日期为2026年8月11日。该县已加强蚊虫控制措施,包括在棕榈港地区增加检查和喷雾作业。登革热主要由埃及伊蚊(Aedes aegypti)和白纹伊蚊(Aedes albopictus)传播,这两种蚊子在白天叮咬,且在小型积水容器中繁殖。卫生官员警告称,当蚊子叮咬感染该病毒的人后,可将病毒传播给他人,从而导致本地登革热传播。目前尚未报告具体死亡或暴露人数,但该县敦促居民清除积水并采取预防措施以减少蚊虫种群。佛罗里达州卫生部继续在全州范围内开展登革热等蚊媒疾病监测,皮内拉斯县则针对活动增加的区域实施有针对性的蚊虫控制措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
皮内拉斯县在超过15年后首次记录本地感染登革热病例,佛罗里达州卫生部于2026年8月11日发布预警。该文章发表于2026年8月24日。
地点与对象
佛罗里达州皮内拉斯县,特别是棕榈港等蚊虫活动增加的地区。受影响人群包括可能在已知登革热传播区域接触蚊虫的居民。
事件
皮内拉斯县确认两例本地感染登革热病例,这是该县15年来首次出现本地登革热传播。病例由佛罗里达州皮内拉斯县卫生部报告。
规模、影响与风险
皮内拉斯县报告两例确诊登革热病例,目前未提供具体死亡或暴露人数。风险在于可能出现本地登革热传播,卫生官员建议居民采取预防措施。
应对、状态与不确定性
皮内拉斯县蚊虫控制部门已在蚊虫活动升高的区域增加检查和喷雾作业。佛罗里达州卫生部继续在全州范围内开展登革热等蚊媒疾病监测。当前情况正在积极调查和应对中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pinellas records first locally acquired dengue cases in more than 15 years - St Pete Catalyst

News Brief
Pinellas County, Florida, has recorded two locally acquired cases of dengue fever, the first in over 15 years, according to the Florida Department of Health. The cases were confirmed on August 11, 2026, and the county has intensified mosquito control efforts, including increased inspections and fogging operations in Palm Harbor. Dengue is primarily transmitted by Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus mosquitoes, which bite during the day and breed in small containers of standing water. Health officials warn that local transmission can occur when a mosquito bites someone infected with the virus and then transmits it to another person. While no specific death or exposure counts have been reported, the county is urging residents to eliminate standing water and take preventive measures to reduce mosquito populations. The Florida Department of Health is continuing statewide surveillance for mosquito-borne illnesses, and Pinellas County is focusing on targeted mosquito-control efforts in areas with increased activity.
Original source text (English)
News Know Innovate Thrive Create Place Impact Venture Arts Content Community Voices Partner Stories Independents The Shuffle Influencers Podcasts Connect Support the Catalyst Contribute Content Promote Your Business Meet the Team Events Connect with us Type a text St Pete Catalyst News Know Innovate Thrive Create Place Impact Venture Arts Content Community Voices Partner Stories Independents The Shuffle Influencers Podcasts Connect Support the Catalyst Contribute Content Promote Your Business Meet the Team Events Thrive Mosquito-borne dengue fever detected in Pinellas Tampa Bay has reports 10 locally acquired cases of the illness. Published 2 days ago on August 24, 2026 By Cora Quantum (AI) The dengue cases come amid broader signs of an active mosquito season in Pinellas. Photo provided. Pinellas County has recorded two locally acquired cases of dengue fever, the county’s first local transmission of the mosquito-borne virus in more than 15 years. The Florida Department of Health in Pinellas County issued a mosquito-borne illness alert Aug. 11 following confirmation of the two cases. Neighboring Hillsborough County has reported eight locally acquired cases, bringing the Tampa Bay total to 10. Pinellas County Mosquito Control has concentrated its response in Palm Harbor, increasing inspections and deploying truck-mounted fogging operations at dawn. County officials said surveillance has shown increased mosquito populations and mosquito-borne virus activity. The dengue cases come amid broader signs of an active mosquito season in Pinellas. Earlier this month, the county reported increased detection of West Nile virus in its sentinel chicken surveillance program. No human West Nile cases had been reported at the time. Recent rainfall has also created abundant mosquito breeding habitat across the county. Mosquito Control has responded with additional truck fogging, aerial larviciding and inspections in areas showing elevated mosquito activity. Dengue is primarily transmitted by Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus mosquitoes. Unlike some mosquito species that are most active around dusk and dawn, these mosquitoes commonly bite during the daytime and frequently breed in small containers of standing water around homes and businesses. Locally acquired cases indicate that transmission occurred within the area rather than during travel to a region where dengue regularly circulates. Local transmission can begin when a mosquito bites someone carrying the virus, who then becomes infected and later transmits dengue to another person. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention says most people infected with dengue have no symptoms or experience mild illness. Symptoms can include fever, headache, pain behind the eyes, muscle and joint pain, nausea, vomiting and rash. Severe dengue can cause internal bleeding, shock and, in rare cases, death. There is no specific antiviral treatment for dengue. Health officials advise people experiencing symptoms, particularly those who may have been exposed to mosquitoes in an area with known transmission, to contact a healthcare provider. Pinellas officials are asking residents to help reduce mosquito populations by eliminating standing water around their properties. Buckets, flowerpots, bird baths, gutters, tarps, toys and other containers can hold enough water for mosquitoes to reproduce. The county recommends its “3 Ds” of mosquito protection: dump standing water, defend against mosquitoes with an EPA-approved repellent and dress in clothing that covers the skin when practical. Residents can also request mosquito-control service through Pinellas County if they are experiencing significant mosquito activity. The Florida Department of Health said it will continue statewide surveillance for mosquito-borne illnesses while Pinellas County continues targeted mosquito-control efforts in areas where increased activity has been detected. 慢性疾病增加登革热风险 - NST Online

Chronic illness raises dengue risk - NST Online

Chronic illness raises dengue risk - NST Online

News Brief
The NST Online headline "Chronic illness raises dengue risk" suggests a possible association between long‐term health conditions and increased vulnerability to dengue infection. However, the extracted article body is unavailable, and none of the supplied evidence provides any details about the claimed link, specific locations, case numbers, or public‐health actions. Consequently, no confirmed, suspected, or preliminary information about dengue risk, affected populations, or response measures can be extracted from this source. Readers should treat the claim as unverified until the full report becomes accessible or additional corroborating sources are identified.
